# PREFACE.

The account book presented here is of great importance, primarily for the history of the city where it was created and where it is now being published <note>This refers to Basel, Switzerland, which became a European hub for printing and humanism in the 15th and 16th centuries.</note>. Even though it belongs to a time that can only be described as "post-classical" for the book trade of Basel—a city once celebrated as the <note>original Latin: "inexhausta librorum mater"</note> *inexhaustible mother of books* (Oswald Bär, 1532)—it nevertheless contains the most abundant and valuable information for this still-significant epoch. However, the account book of those two great booksellers should be recognized as a source of the first rank, not only for the history of Basel but for the entire German book trade. In this regard, a comparison is invited, on the one hand, with Michael Harder’s <term>Fair Memorial<note>A "Messmemorial" was a merchant's private ledger used to record stock, sales, and debts specifically during the major trade fairs in cities like Frankfurt or Leipzig.</note></term> (edited by Kelchner and Wülcker, Frankfurt am Main, 1873), and on the other hand, with the general fair catalogs (compiled and edited by G. Schwetschke as the <note>original Latin: "Codex nundinarius Germaniæ"</note> *Registry of German Fairs*, Halle, Schwetschke, 1850), to both of which records it

<summary>This preface introduces a 16th-century bookseller's account book from Basel, emphasizing its value as a primary source for the history of the European book trade and comparing it to other major trade fair records.</summary>
<keywords>Basel, book trade history, account books, Michael Harder, trade fairs, 16th century, bibliography, printing history</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's introduction, comparing the current account book to other historical records and detailing the editorial principles used for the transcription.</meta>

precedes [those other records] by only a few years. But how much more comprehensive and penetrating it is at the same time than that record, which only indicates the one-time turnover of a smaller trader who was not active as a printer, and how much more insightful than those others, which contribute nothing to our understanding of the internal workings and operations of a bookshop! —

In this edition, the principle of fully maintaining the transmitted form has been applied to the treatment of the text, as we are dealing not with official deeds or administrative records, but with a document of a personal character. However, this did not exclude changes of a purely graphic nature, and in this regard, the necessary steps were taken: capital letters are used only for proper names and at the beginning of a section; the letters 'u' and 'v' are used according to their actual phonetic value; 'uu' is rendered as 'w'; umlauts are indicated by two dots placed above the letter in German words, and by an adjacent 'e' in Latin words; and diphthongs are indicated by <term>stacked letters<note>original Latin: "literæ columnatæ"; a typographic practice where one letter is placed directly above another to represent a diphthong or abbreviation.</note></term>.

Following the reproduction of the account book is a brief treatise on the individuals and circumstances of the bookselling partnership which the account book served. From the abundance of what could have been noted, discussed, or detailed here, only a few important matters have been addressed. This is followed by a series of explanatory notes, organized according to the pages and lines of the text. However, from the lists of names provided during the various trade fairs, only the residents of Basel have been mentioned here. Books whose titles appear in the text were omitted from these notes if it was not possible for me to locate physical copies of them. An index of persons and places concludes this small volume.

<summary>The editor explains the editorial standards for the transcription, including modernizing certain typographic conventions while preserving the document's personal character, and outlines the supplemental scholarly materials included in the volume.</summary>
<keywords>editorial methods, paleography, typography, Basel booksellers, trade fair lists, archival transcription, 16th-century bookkeeping</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Rudolf Wackernagel’s introduction. The original manuscript of the account book was, until now, held as a highly valued and significant possession by <term>Government Councilor<note>German: *Regierungsrath*; a title for a high-ranking civil servant or member of a regional executive body.</note></term> Dr. Gottlieb Bischoff in Basel, a descendant of the elder Nicolaus Episcopius <note>The Latinized form of the surname "Bischoff" (meaning "Bishop"). Nicolaus Episcopius (1501–1564) was a prominent Basel printer and son-in-law to Johannes Froben.</note>. He most kindly offered it to me for publication, for which I wish to express my most sincere gratitude here. The manuscript will be handed over to the local public library. I am particularly indebted to the head of the library, Dr. L. Sieber, for his extensive assistance.

Basel, April 1881.

Rudolf Wackernagel.

IV

<warning>The text on this page consists of faint, mirrored bleed-through from the reverse side of the paper. The transcription reflects the text in its intended reading orientation.</warning>

<image-desc>A faint, mirrored woodcut illustration from the preceding page is visible in the center, depicting a standing male figure dressed in long scholarly or clerical robes.</image-desc>

The original manuscript of the <term>arithmetic book<note>German: *Rechenbuch*. In this context, it refers to the influential mathematical textbooks of Adam Ries, which helped standardize arithmetic in the German-speaking world.</note></term> was until now considered lost and only became known in its original form when it came into the hands of the editor in the year 1891, after it was most kindly offered to the editor for publication in the year 1890 from Annaberg in the <term>Ore Mountains<note>German: *Erzgebirge*; a mountain range and historical mining region on the border of Saxony and Bohemia where Adam Ries spent much of his life.</note></term>. At this point, my most sincere thanks should be expressed. The manuscript will be handed over to the local public library. I am particularly indebted to the head of the latter, Professor Dr. Stehlin, for his assistance in many ways.

Basel, in April 1892.

->Rudolf Wackernagel.<-

# THE ACCOUNT BOOK

->OF THE<-

# FROBEN & EPISCOPIUS

->1557 — 1564.<-

<image-desc>Printer's mark of the Froben press: A caduceus held by two hands reaching out from clouds at the base. Two crowned serpents are entwined around the central staff, with their heads facing each other. A small bird is perched on the very top of the staff. The name of the printer is split across the center of the image.</image-desc>

<note>The printer's mark featuring the caduceus (the staff of Hermes) was the famous emblem of the Froben press. The serpents represent wisdom and the bird represents simplicity, symbolizing the firm's motto, "Wise simplicity." Hieronymus Froben and Nicolaus Episcopius were the successors to Johann Froben, the great humanist printer and close friend of Erasmus of Rotterdam.</note>

->FRO BEN<-

<vocab>Account book, Froben, Episcopius, Basel, printer's mark, 16th century</vocab>

### ¶ ACCOUNT OF THE LENT FAIR 1558

<margin>5</margin>
BETWEEN THE FROBENS AND THE EPISCOPIUSES, <note>The Froben and Episcopius families were the two most prominent printing dynasties in Basel, often operating as a single business syndicate.</note> concerning the printing trade, including all income and expenditures in Basel and Frankfurt, from September 1557 until Laetare 1558, <note>Laetare is the fourth Sunday of Lent; the fair cycles followed the liturgical calendar.</note> concluded on May 9, 1558.

¶ FROM September 1557 until LAETARE 1558, the following costs were incurred at "The Air" <note>original German: "zum Lufft," referring to the Haus zum Luft, the famous Basel workshop of the Froben family.</note> 
<margin>10</margin>
for the Frobens and Episcopiuses to maintain the shared printing works.

| | lb. <note>lb. (Lira/Pounds).</note> | sh. <note>sh. (Schillings).</note> | dn. <note>dn. (Denarii/Pence).</note> |

My heart is struck like grass and withered, for I have forgotten to eat my bread. By reason of my groaning, my bones cling to my flesh; I am like a pelican of the desert. <note>Original Latin: "Percuſus ſum ut foenum et aruit cor meum, quia oblitus ſum conmedere panem meum. a voce gemitus mei adheſit os meum carni meæ, ſimilis factus ſum pelicano ſolitudinis." These verses are from Psalm 102:4–6, a common meditative inclusion in ledgers of this period.</note>

> I have become like a night owl in the house. I have kept watch and have become like a solitary sparrow on the roof. My enemies reproached me all day long, and those who praised me swore against me. <note>original: "Factus ſum ſicut nycticorax in domicilio..." This is a quotation from the Latin Vulgate, Psalm 102:6-8 (Psalm 101 in the period's numbering). It was common for merchants and printers to begin ledger pages or new sections with a pious or reflective verse.</note>

| Total of all expenditures in common at Frankfurt and at Basel for this 1562 Autumn Fair | 3387 | 20 | 11 |

Thus, Episcopius has spent more than Frobenius by 283 fl. 11 sh. 1 d., excluding the 25 fl. 5 sh. 10 d. which were taken from the common funds at Frankfurt. A 1/2 share of this difference amounts to 141 fl. 12 sh. 8 d.

Remaining balance that Frobenius owes to Episcopius: 9 fl. 14 sh. 10 dn.

> And my spirit was overwhelmed within me; my heart within me was troubled. ## ¶ ACCOUNT OF THE LENT FAIR 1564
### BETWEEN THE EPISCOPIUS AND FROBEN FAMILIES.

<margin>
¶ On March 7, 1564, my dearest father, Lord <note>original: "d." for "dominus"</note> Nicolaus Episcopius, died.
</margin>
CONCERNING THE COMMON BUSINESS OF THE PRINTING HOUSE including all expenses and income in Basel and Frankfurt. After the passing of both our parents, and at the conclusion of this account, the partnership with the Froben family has ended and is finished. This account was closed on May 6, 1564, by the will of Master Jacob Ryedens, who wished to divide our interests from one another.

<meta>This page serves as a physical description and introduction to the manuscript containing the financial records of the Episcopius printing house in Basel. It establishes the physical state of the document, noting its dimensions, the materials used in its binding (repurposed 15th-century parchment), and the significant gaps in the surviving text compared to the original pagination.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut vignette at the top of the page. In the center is a shield bearing the coat of arms of Basel (the Baselstab), flanked by two basilisks. Above them is a banner with the inscription "IN CLYTA BASILEA" <note>Latin: "In Renowned Basel"</note>. To the left of the shield, an angel or winged figure sits at a desk with writing implements and books. To the right, an owl wearing a mortarboard or halo sits atop a stack of large volumes. The signature "KNAUSS sc." <note>The abbreviation "sc." stands for the Latin *sculpsit*, meaning "he engraved this."</note> is visible in the lower left corner of the illustration.</image-desc>

The manuscript, a transcription of which is provided on the preceding pages, is a large folio notebook (26 by 37 cm), consisting of thick paper with the watermark of a hanging hunting horn, wrapped in two leaves of a 15th-century legal parchment manuscript. <note>It was common practice in the early modern period to reuse older, "obsolete" parchment manuscripts—such as medieval legal texts—as durable covers for newer paper accounts.</note>

It is written in delicate, clean strokes, here and there with red ink, and was also paginated by the hand of the scribe himself. <gloss>1</gloss>

Unfortunately, only the smaller part of its original volume has been preserved for us; out of 62 leaves <note><term>leaf</term>: a single sheet of paper or parchment, comprising two pages (front and back).</note>, only 21 remain. Of these surviving leaves, the first is blank on both sides and unpaginated; likewise, the back side of the last leaf is left empty and not counted. Thus, the beginning and the end of the manuscript are still present.

The pagination goes up to 121, though now of course interrupted by many gaps: pages 5–30 and 33–48 are missing,

---

<gloss>1</gloss> This is evident from the references to earlier leaves <note>original: "Folia"</note> frequently contained in the text, which clearly always correspond. However, the fact that the scribe still entered the last part of the 1557 autumn account <note>original: "Herbstrechnung"</note> on page 1 occurred because this final part of the account (see below, page 93) always found its final settlement only in the following account.

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 77, which described the physical condition, watermark, and missing leaves of the manuscript. This page explains the editor's modern pagination system and introduces the historical figures involved in the printing partnership.</meta>

53–88, 113–118. To achieve a continuous sequence, I have paginated the pages anew, while also providing the previous page number in each instance.

In this new <term>pagination</term> <note>The system of numbering pages in a book.</note>, however, two pages now correspond to one of the old ones; because it seemed desirable to retain the page divisions of the manuscript, but the format of the printed edition was insufficient for this, a single page of the manuscript was divided in each case and reproduced on two facing pages of the book. Deviations from this occur on pages 2 and 3, and 4 and 5, where the manuscript page contained too much, and page 17, where it contained too little.

So much for the physical form of the manuscript. But what is its content?

<margin>Content of the manuscript.</margin>
At first glance, it tells us that it is an account book—specifically, an account book relating to a printing and bookselling business operated by a <term>partnership</term> <note>Original: *Societæt*. In the 16th century, large printing houses often operated as a "societas" or legal partnership between families or investors.</note>.

The more precise details that this account book provides regarding the circumstances of this partnership and the business conducted by it will be mentioned below. For now, we are concerned with the various persons who are to be recognized as members of the association, namely:

<margin>The partners.</margin>
Hieronymus Froben <note>Son of the famous printer Johann Froben and a close friend of Erasmus of Rotterdam.</note> with his sons Ambrosius and Aurelius, and Nicolaus Episcopius with his sons Nicolaus and Eusebius.

<summary>This page explains the editor's method for re-paginating the manuscript for modern publication and introduces the primary subject: a 16th-century account book belonging to the Froben and Episcopius printing partnership.</summary>
<keywords>pagination, account book, partnership, Hieronymus Froben, Nicolaus Episcopius, Basel printing, business records</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the introduction of the account book's partners, this page begins a biographical section on the primary figure of the printing house, Hieronymus Froben, detailing his family background and early career.</meta>

# I. Hieronymus Froben.

He was the son of Johannes Froben <note>A famous printer and close friend of the scholar Erasmus.</note> and Gertrud Lachner. Born August 6, 1501,^1 he is said to have been the first person born in Basel after it joined the Swiss Confederation, and was supposedly held at the baptismal font by the ambassadors themselves.^2

He began practicing his father's profession at an early age; books were already appearing under his name by 1520.^3 After Johannes’s death, he entered into a partnership with Johannes Herwagen, his stepfather, and soon also with Nicolaus Episcopius, his brother-in-law; from 1531 onward, he was associated only with the latter and remained so until his death. A proud series of the most beautiful printed works owes its existence to this union; the <term>account book</term> <note>A ledger tracking the expenses and profits of the printing business.</note> published here also pertains to this partnership.

On June 2, 1521, Hieronymus renewed his father's membership in the <term>Saffron Guild</term> <note>Original: *Zunft zu Safran*. One of the elite merchant guilds in Basel, traditionally associated with spice traders and high-level commerce.</note>; however, he does not seem to have taken a very active part in its communal life: he held office for only 3 years, starting in 1534

---

^1 According to the epitaph <note>A commemorative inscription on a tombstone.</note>, which is printed correctly in Wurstisen's *Epitome* 117 (1577 edition), but incorrectly (LVI instead of LXI) in Gross 77 and Tonjola 19.
^2 Cf. the epitaph: <note>original: "inter initia federis Helveti Basileæ nato"</note> "born in Basel at the beginning of the Swiss confederation."
^3 Reber (and Stockmeyer), *Contributions to the History of Basel Printing* 115.

<summary>This page introduces Hieronymus Froben, detailing his birth as a "child of the Swiss Confederation" and the start of his prolific career in the Basel printing industry alongside Herwagen and Episcopius.</summary>
<keywords>Hieronymus Froben, Johannes Froben, Saffron Guild, Basel history, printing history, Nicolaus Episcopius, Johannes Herwagen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Hieronymus Froben, specifically detailing his civic duties in Basel, his two marriages to Anna Lachner and Barbara Brand, his children, and his residence at the house "Zum Luft" where he hosted the famous scholar Erasmus.</meta>

until 1537, the subordinate office of a delegate for flood emergencies; from then on, he is no longer mentioned at all. ¹

He also did not participate in public life, and no appointment to office shows his name. His activity was dedicated exclusively to his profession.

He was married in his first marriage to Anna Lachner, likely a relative on his mother’s side. This first wife ² was still living in August of the year 1538. ³ Her successor was Barbara Brand, the daughter of Mayor Theodor Brand; she seems to have died before Hieronymus.

The children of these marriages were:

1. Hieronymus, born 1532, baptized on April 11 at St. Martin’s. ⁴

2. Ambrosius.

3. Aurelius.

4. Anna, born 1541, baptized March 22 at St. Martin’s ⁵; her godparents bear famous names: Joannes Bebelius, the wife of Conrad Rösch <note>original: "uxor Conradi Rösch"</note>, Catherina Holbein <note>Likely related to the famous painter Hans Holbein the Younger, who was active in Basel and a friend of the Froben circle.</note>. She became the wife of Hans Ludwig Meyer. ⁶

Hieronymus lived in the house "Arow" even during his father's lifetime and initially kept it after the latter's death; not until 1531 did he sell it and acquire the house "Zum Luft" <note>Literal: "The House of Air/Breeze." This house became a center of European intellectual life.</note>. ⁷ Here he remained until his death, here he established his <term>printing house</term> <note>original: "Officin." A term used for a printer's workshop, derived from the Latin *officina*.</note>, and here he hosted Erasmus.

Hieronymus Froben seems to have been of a sickly constitution. As early as 1552, the younger Nicolaus Episcopius wrote of

him, who was only 52 years old: *he is somewhat failing and growing old* <note>original: "aliquantulum deficit atque senescit"</note>¹; his portrait² shows him in 1557 as a man with already completely whitened hair³, and his epitaph laments: — *in the most bitter and long-lasting torments of the stomach, by which he was also extinguished.* <note>original: "in acerbissimis ac diuturnis stomachi cruciatibus quibus et extinctus est." This suggests he likely died of a chronic gastric illness.</note>

He died on March 13, 1563, and was buried in the Minster <note>The Basel Cathedral.</note>. His gravestone stands there in the small cloister, next to the stones of Isengrin and Guarinus <note>Michael Isengrin and Thomas Guarinus were also prominent Basel printers, showing that even in death, these masters of the press remained together.</note>.

## II. Ambrosius Froben.

This son of Hieronymus and Anna Lachner was born in 1537 and baptized on October 30 at St. Martin’s.⁴

On March 14, 1557, he entered the <term>Saffron Guild</term> <note>original: "Safranzunft." In Basel, this guild represented merchants, including those in the book trade.</note>; in 1562 he became the <term>steward</term> <note>original: "Stubenmeister." An administrative official responsible for the guild’s meeting house.</note> here, and in 1568 a <term>member of the council of six</term> <note>original: "Sechser." A representative of the guild in the city's government.</note>, and so forth.⁵

He was married to Salomea, the daughter of the <term>Chief Guild Master</term> <note>original: "Oberstzunftmeister." One of the highest civic offices in Basel.</note> Jacob Rüdin (baptized September 7, 1539, at St. Peter’s).⁶

He too was a printer and bookseller, initially, it seems, in partnership with his father and the Episcopius family, and later alone with his brother Aurelius.⁷

## III. Aurelius Froben.

Regarding the life of this man as well, I have only very meager reports.

The year of his birth is uncertain; however, he must have been younger than Ambrosius, as he renewed his membership in the Saffron Guild two years after him, on December 4, 1559.⁸

<meta>This page continues the genealogy of the Froben family, briefly concluding the section on Aurelius Froben before transitioning to a detailed biography of his partner and brother-in-law, Nicolaus Episcopius the Elder. It explores the debate surrounding Episcopius’s place of origin.</meta>

He was married to Chrischona Burckhardt, daughter of Christoph.

As previously mentioned, he also dedicated himself to the book trade.

### IV. Nicolaus Episcopius the Elder

He was born in the same year as his associate Hieronymus [Froben], 1501.1 However, his place of birth is not certain. While Montdidier in Bresse <note>A region in eastern France, near the Swiss border.</note> is usually named as his home—as it was by Schweighauser,2 and following him, Reber,3—this is by no means established. It is supported only by the account of Guichenon in his *History of Bresse and Bugey, Gex and Valromey* (Lyon 1650, Folio) p. 30: *Nicolas l'Evesque, Episcopius in Latin, a learned and accurate printer of his time who gave the public a quantity of rare books, was from the land of Montdidier in Bresse and retired to Basel where he married the daughter of Froben; he is buried in Basel with this epitaph which I saw there: To Nicolaus Episcopius of the Sebusiani.* <note>original French: "Nicolas l'Evesque... étoit de la terre de Montdidier en Bresse..." The term "Sebusiani" refers to an ancient Celtic tribe of the Bresse region, reinforcing the claim of his French origin.</note>

Further evidence for this opinion may be found in the verses which Erasmus, upon his departure for Freiburg in 1529, jokingly attached to a farewell gift of a rooster and a hen sent to Episcopius:

> *You are a Gaul [a rooster] and you have a hen; as a lucky omen I give*
> *A rooster, whose anxious wife tends the tender chicks.*4
<note>original Latin: "Gallus es et gallina tibi est..." Erasmus is playing on the double meaning of the Latin word "Gallus," which means both "a rooster" and "a Frenchman/Gaul."</note>

Here, Episcopius is called a *Gallus*, which indeed aligns well with the assumption that he was born in Montdidier.

---

1 Epitaph in Wurstisen's *Epitome* 180, Gross 143, Tonjola 120.
2 *Collectanea* [Collected Notes] on the history of Basel book printers.
3 *Contributions* 117.
4 *Works* (Leiden 1703) III, 1224, D. Or could it be that Erasmus did not intend a pun at all?

<summary>This page introduces Nicolaus Episcopius the Elder, a prominent Basel printer and associate of the Froben family, focusing on the historical evidence and literary puns by Erasmus that suggest he was originally from Montdidier in Bresse, France.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Episcopius the Elder, Erasmus of Rotterdam, Montdidier, Bresse, Basel printing history, book trade, Latin puns, Sebusiani</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the French origin theory of Nicolaus Episcopius the Elder to evidence suggesting an Alsatian origin. It cites local Basel administrative records—citizenship registries and guild entries—to prove he hailed from Rittershofen near Wissembourg, rather than Bresse.</meta>

But there are decisive testimonies opposing this assumption, which consistently attribute another home to Episcopius. These are the documentations of his entry into citizenship and the guild, and his epitaph.

> 1. Item, Mr. Nicholas Bischoff of Rittershofen <note>A village in Alsace, near Wissembourg.</note> has purchased <term>burgher rights</term> <note>The legal status of a citizen in a city, granting specific economic and political privileges.</note> for 4 guilders and has paid in cash on the Feast of St. Paul's Conversion in the year [15]20 (January 25, 1520). <note>Footnote 1</note>
> 2. On Monday after the Purification of Mary in the year [15]20 (February 6, 1520), burgher rights were granted to Nicholas Bischoff of Rittershofen, and he swore the oath according to custom. <note>Footnote 2 & 3; original Latin: "et juravit pro ut moris est."</note>
> 3. On Friday after Misericordia <note>The second Sunday after Easter.</note> in the year 1529 (April 16, 1529), the honorable Nicholas Bischoff of Rittershofen, the printer, appeared before my lords, the councilors, the master, and the Six <note>The governing board of a guild.</note>, and asked them for membership in their honorable Saffron Guild; my lords have granted him such guild membership according to the decree and ordinance of my lords; it was granted to him for 4 pounds and 14 shillings, which my lords have been paid in cash, and this occurred during the time of Mr. Hans Irmy, then-Master of the Saffron Guild. <note>Footnote 4</note>
> 4. Epitaph: To Nicolaus Episcopius, the Sebusian. <note>original Latin: "Nicolao Episcopio Sebusiano."</note>

*Sebusium* or *Sebusia* is Wissembourg in Alsace; and the interpretation of the place mentioned in items 1–3 as Rittershofen fits this perfectly, as it is today a village in the district of Wissembourg, Canton of Soultz. One must assume that this was Episcopius's true home, and that the epitaph only used the nearby city’s name as a general identifier. <note>Footnote 5</note>

Episcopius, therefore, must have been born in Rittershofen.

---

<note>Footnote 1</note> Protocol on citizenship fees 106.
<note>Footnote 2</note> Marcus Lutz read "Reiterschafften" here, *Citizens' Book* 57.
<note>Footnote 3</note> *Opening Book* VII, 179.
<note>Footnote 4</note> Admission register of the Saffron Guild II, 59.
<note>Footnote 5</note> Horawitz, *General German Biography* VI, 155, has—unlike Schweighauser and Reber—used the information from the epitaph as evidence for the man's origin and thus almost arrived at the whole truth.

<summary>This page presents primary source evidence from Basel's city and guild records to argue that the printer Nicolaus Episcopius was originally from Rittershofen in Alsace, rather than France. It reconciles his Latin epitaph ("the Sebusian") with the proximity of his home village to the city of Wissembourg.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Episcopius, Rittershofen, Wissembourg, Alsace, Saffron Guild, citizenship, burgher rights, epitaph, Sebusium, Basel history</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into Nicolaus Episcopius’s origins, attempting to reconcile his Alsatian birth with contemporary accounts suggesting French ties, particularly those of Erasmus and the historian Guichenon.</meta>

How then can we reconcile the poem by Erasmus (assuming it is interpreted correctly) and the claims made by Guichenon, both of which must surely point to some historical truth? Perhaps one can do justice to every report by assuming that Episcopius was indeed born in Rittershofen and first traveled from there to Basel in 1520, but afterwards made a perhaps lengthy stay in Montdidier;¹ such a stay would explain why Erasmus called him a Frenchman (see above) and why Guichenon later assigned him to that place;² above all, this also makes his very late entry into the <term>guild</term> <note>A professional association of craftsmen or merchants; in Basel, printers were usually required to join a guild to practice their trade.</note> understandable.

When Nicolaus Episcopius arrived in Basel, he did not enter a city of strangers. On the contrary, relatives from his father's side lived there, most notably his cousin, the prominent Andreas Bischoff, surnamed "of Hiltalingen";³ these relatives were likely the reason he settled in the city of his ancestors. Admittedly, it remains unexplained who Nicolaus Episcopius’s father was, and under what circumstances Nicolaus came into the world in the village of Rittershofen. It is to be hoped that documentary evidence regarding this will yet be found; at this hour, such records are not at my disposal.

In 1529, or shortly before, Nicolaus came to Basel, this time never to leave again. In the aforementioned year—

---

¹ In 1526 he was perhaps in Paris. See Erasmus's letter to Tussanus, *Works, volume III, 938*.
² In any case, it is strange that Guichenon reproduces the description of Episcopius in the epitaph as "Sebusian" <note>original: *Sebusianus*; referring to Sebusium or Wissembourg in Alsace.</note>—which actually contradicts his own statement—without any further comment.
³ Born 1481, son of Claus Bischoff and Anna Grienenzwig, married to Barbara Bär (*Beinheim Chronicle, Manuscript of the Public Library of Basel, Folio 3*), <term>Councilman</term> <note>original: *Ratsherr*; a member of the city’s governing council.</note> for the "Bear" guild, <term>District Governor</term> <note>original: *Landvogt*; a representative of the city's authority in rural territories.</note> at Münchenstein, <term>Member of the Board of Three</term> <note>original: *Dreierherr*; a municipal official overseeing financial and tax matters.</note>; as a follower of the old religion <note>Meaning Roman Catholicism, during the period of the Reformation.</note>, he was removed from the Council in 1529, turned toward Freiburg, and died there in 1558 without male offspring; he gave his farm Hiltalingen and his letter of armory to his "father's brother's grandson" *Eusebius Bischoff* (see below). (*Bischoff Papers.*)

...he won for himself a wife, Justina Frobenia, the 17-year-old surviving daughter of the great Johannes Froben <note>Johannes Froben (c. 1460–1527) was one of the most famous printers of the Renaissance and a close friend of Erasmus.</note>:

> "I heartily congratulated both of you that this sacred bond of marriage has joined such a well-matched pair: a chaste man to a chaste woman, a pure man to a pure woman, an upright man to an upright woman; I hope that I may soon congratulate you even more, when a little 'Little Bishop' <note>original: *Episcopiolus*; a punning diminutive of the groom's surname, Episcopius, which means 'Bishop' in Latin</note> plays in your courtyard, etc."

wrote Erasmus to him from Freiburg. <gloss>1</gloss>

Nicolaus and Justina lived in the House "zum Sessel" [at the Chair] (I hear that you will move into the house <note>original: *audio te successurum in ædes, quas isthic meo discessu feci vacuas*</note> which I left vacant there upon my departure <gloss>2</gloss>), likely together with Herwagen and his wife, the widow of Froben and Justina's mother.

With him, as well as with Hieronymus Froben, Nicolaus began a joint book business; Herwagen left this partnership in 1531, while the two brothers-in-law remained together for life. <gloss>3</gloss>

In the year 1520, Nicolaus Episcopius had become a citizen; <gloss>10</gloss> however, quite a long time passed before he entered into the activities of public life. From 1540 to 1542, he is mentioned as an assessor at the <term>Marriage Court</term> <note>original: *Ehegericht*; a civic body that oversaw matrimonial disputes, moral conduct, and divorces in post-Reformation Basel.</note>; from 1548 onward as an assessor at the <term>Magistrate’s Court</term> <note>original: *Schultheissengericht*; the court of the Schultheiss or bailiff, handling civil and minor criminal matters.</note>; and from 1551 onward as a <term>Bannherr</term> <note>A high-ranking administrative official, often responsible for district oversight or managing church property on behalf of the city.</note> at St. Peter’s. <gloss>11</gloss>

We are more precisely informed about the position Episcopius held within the circle of his guild brothers, although this activity was also not very extensive.

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Nicolaus Episcopius, detailing his late-career transition from private business into public service and guild leadership in Basel, ending with the tragic death of his family during the plague.</meta>

In 1529, he joined the <term>Saffron Guild</term> <note>original: *Zunft zu Safran*; one of the most prestigious guilds in Basel, representing merchants, spice traders, and eventually many of the city's intellectual elite.</note>¹ and was appointed to the following positions here:
1534 Official for flood protection,
1541 <term>Chamber Master</term> <note>original: *Stubenmeister*; a senior administrative official within the guild.</note> of the trades,
1555 <term>Sixer</term> <note>original: *Sechser*; a member of the guild’s representative council of six, who also served as a representative in the city’s Great Council.</note>, an office he held until his death,
1555 and 1560 also Chamber Master of the Sixers,
1557–1559 Official for the <term>Spalen Gate</term> <note>original: *Spalenthor*; a major gatehouse in Basel's fortifications; this role involved oversight of the district or the gate's defense.</note>,
1560–1563 Official at the <term>Artillery Depot</term> <note>original: *Werkhof zum Geschütz*; the city arsenal responsible for maintaining cannons and military equipment.</note>.²

Thus, it was only toward the end of his life that he participated to some extent in public and guild activities.

In his earlier years, during the peak of his strength, he lived primarily for his profession. The fact that he later devoted himself to other forms of activity seems to have been due to the fact that his sons were now entering the business and taking over a portion of the work.

Nicolaus Episcopius was 63 years old when he died of <term>consumption</term> <note>original: *Schwindsucht*; a term historically used for wasting diseases, most often tuberculosis.</note>³ on March 7, 1564.⁴ Half a year later, on September 27, his widow died of the plague along with their children Hieronymus, Justina, Ursula, and Maria. They were laid in Nicolaus’s grave at St. Peter’s, and a marble⁵ tablet covers the father, mother, and children.

He came into the world in the year 1531, as the first-born of Nicolaus and Justina.

Even in his early youth, he dedicated himself to his father's profession <note>The printing trade, which was a central pillar of Basel's intellectual and economic life in the 16th century.</note>, and as early as the year 1553, books appeared printed under his name.¹

At the beginning of that same year, 1553, he married Elisabeth Peyer, daughter of Mr. Hans Heinrich Peyer of Schaffhausen,² likely a relative of the Daniel Peyer who married Nicolaus's sister Modestia two years later.³

The children of Nicolaus and Elisabeth were:

1. A daughter who died shortly after birth in the autumn of 1553.⁴
2. Nicolaus, born in 1555 and baptized on May 2 at St. Peter’s <note>St. Peter’s Church in Basel, a common site for the baptisms of the city’s prominent printing families.</note>; among the baptismal witnesses were Michael Isengrin and Maria Oporina.⁵ This Nicolaus married Judith Burckhardt in 1580 and died as early as October 1582.⁶ It was not he who distinguished himself as a statesman, as Horawitz noted in the work cited above <note>original: *a. a. O.* (am angegebenen Ort)</note>, but rather his son of the same name. He himself was only a printer.
3. Agnes, born in 1558 and baptized on February 2 at St. Peter’s.⁷
4. Justina, born in 1561 and baptized on February 28 at St. Peter’s;⁸ she married Johann Brombach in 1579 and died on August 22, 1608.⁹

<meta>This page continues the biography of the younger Nicolaus Episcopius, detailing his guild involvement, his death during a plague outbreak in Basel, and the remarriage of his widow. It then begins the biographical entry for the next family member, Eusebius Episcopius.</meta>

he seems to have distinguished himself among his fellow guild members. On June 28, 1551, he renewed his guild rights at the Saffron Guild <term>Safran (Saffron Guild): One of the influential merchant guilds in Basel, which historically represented merchants and certain trades like printers.</term>. His name appears only once in the guild’s administrative records, in 1556, where he is listed as a <term>Stubenmeister (Guild Master): An official responsible for the administration of the guild house or "Stube".</term> of the trades; otherwise, his name is not to be found. Like his father, he appears to have dedicated his undivided energy to his profession, and an early death took him before he reached old age.

He died as early as the year 1565, on December 29, <gloss>1</gloss> at the age of 34, from the plague.

That this was the cause of his death is explicitly attested by Wurstisen, <gloss>2</gloss> and this is corroborated by Felix Plater <note>A famous Swiss physician and professor in Basel, known for his detailed medical journals and observations of plague outbreaks.</note>, who in his description of the pestilence <gloss>3</gloss> lists a "Nicolaus Episcopius, master printer" <note>original: *truckerher* (Druckerherr)</note> among the deceased. <gloss>4</gloss>

Nicolaus was buried at St. Peter’s.

His widow, Elisabeth Peyer, later married the jurist Samuel Grynäus, son of Simon <note>The Grynäus family was a cornerstone of Basel's intellectual life; Simon was a famed humanist and theologian.</note>.

He was also a book printer and book merchant, usually in partnership with his relatives; in 1568, he acquired the Herwagen workshop <note>The Herwagen (or Hervagius) press was one of the most famous printing houses in Basel, founded by Johann Herwagen the Elder.</note>. <gloss>2</gloss>

From his father's cousin, Mr. Andreas Bischoff, he received the Hiltalingen estate <note>Located just outside Basel, this property elevated the family's social status.</note> as a gift, which is why his descendants carried the name "Bischoff of Hiltalingen." <gloss>3</gloss>

His wife died on October 21, 1577, <gloss>4</gloss> and he himself died on October 5, 1599. <gloss>5</gloss> Both lie buried at St. Peter’s <note>A significant church in Basel that served as a burial site for many of the city's prominent humanist and merchant families.</note>.

Following this is a brief summary of what can be gathered from the account book regarding its own nature, the person of the scribe, and the organization of the partnership.

<margin>Character of the account book.</margin> And here we are primarily concerned with the question: is the account book a private record made for personal purposes, or is it the business ledger of the firm itself?

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the account book's nature, following the introduction to the partners (Gemeinder) of the printing firm. It argues that the book is an official business ledger rather than a private notebook.</meta>

I do not hesitate to affirm the latter. This is indicated by the manner of drafting and the entire method of calculation: the precise listing of every item—even the smallest—spent by both parties, and the completed balancing and reconciliation of their mutual claims. To highlight specific details, for example, the sentence on page 51 points toward this: "this is all settled with the Episcopiuses and Frobeniuses" <note>original: "dysess ist alles verrechnet cum Episcopiis et Frobeniis"</note>; here the scribe clearly appears as the accountant and cashier of the partnership. The only thing that might lead to the opposite opinion is the fact that, at the conclusion of the accounts, usually only the personal share of the scribe is calculated and listed in the final line. This is explained by the fact that the division of the business result was considered only as a split between the two groups—the Frobenii and Episcopii, or rather, between the two fathers—and was established in the account through the mutual reconciliation of the income and expenses incurred by both sides. In what way the distribution then proceeded further within each family was irrelevant to the partnership’s treasury; therefore, the inclusion of the drafter's personal share was indeed unnecessary but also not at all disruptive.

We must therefore regard the book as an actual business ledger <term>Handlungsbuch: A formal commercial account book or business ledger used to track the transactions, debts, and credits of a firm.</term> of the firm,¹ through which its content gains authenticity and its significance increases in scale.

<margin>The scribe of the account book.</margin>
But who was the scribe of the book? It was one of the partners <term>Gemeinder: A member of a "Gesellschaft" or business partnership, sharing in the risks and profits of the printing enterprise.</term> himself; this is clearly evident from the passage: "so [he] wanted to divide *us*" (page 72, line 15), while on the other hand, passages such as page 2, line 8ff. and page 14, line 11: "he gave to us" <note>original: "dedit nobis"</note>, page 66, line 15: "*our landlord*",

---

¹ Naturally, it was not the firm's only book. Alongside it, mention is also made of a journal (page 10, line 8), a fair-book <note>original: "Messbuch," referring to a ledger used specifically to track sales and inventory at the major international book fairs like those in Frankfurt or Basel.</note> (page 49, line 5), and a register (page 9, line 16; 30, 20; 75, 27).

<summary>This page argues that the document is an official business ledger for the Froben-Episcopius printing partnership, citing its precise accounting of shared expenses and the scribe's role as a partner and cashier.</summary>
<keywords>account book, Handlungsbuch, business ledger, Froben family, Episcopius family, bookkeeping, Basel printing, partnership accounting</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the identity of the account book's scribe, building on the previous conclusion that the book served as an official ledger for the printing firm. It uses internal genealogical references and financial shares to pinpoint the specific family member responsible for the record-keeping.</meta>

Pages 2, 5. 14, 6: "the debtors have received books from *us*" <note>original: "die Schuldner haben Bücher von uns empfangen"</note>, would not be sufficient on its own to reach this conclusion.

He was, furthermore, a son of Nicolaus Episcopius the elder; see "my father Nicolaus Episcopius the elder" <note>original: "myn vatter Nicolaus Episcopius pater"</note>, see pages 9, 22; 70, 10; 72, 8.; furthermore "brother-in-law Daniel" (namely Peyer): page 49, 3; "my cousin Hieronymus Frobenius": page 7, 20.

And indeed, it was the elder of these sons, Nicolaus: "I Nicolaus": page 9, 25; to this is added the comparison of those passages where the scribe indicates 1/8 as his share: page 25, 19; 31, 16–20; 47, 14; 48, 33; 61, 18; 64, 29; 65, 25, compared with page 2, 6: "of which sum, Nicolaus Episcopius the son's share is the eighth part." <gloss>1</gloss>

Nicolaus Episcopius, the son of Nicolaus, was the bookkeeper and likely also the treasurer <term>Seckelmeister: A traditional Swiss and South German title for a treasurer or financial officer responsible for a "Seckel" (purse/treasury).</term> of the partnership; as such, he wrote the present book. He organized it in the following manner:

<margin>Organization of the account book.</margin>
The individual accounts <gloss>2</gloss> are prepared for the Lenten or the Autumn Fair <note>The Frankfurt Trade Fair was the most important event for the European book trade, held twice a year.</note> at Frankfurt and extend over the period of the most recently passed half-year. Each of these accounts is regularly divided into seven groups:
a. Printing expenses for "The Air" <note>Referring to the house "zum Luft," one of the firm's printing offices.</note>.
b. Printing expenses for "The Chair" <note>Referring to the house "zum Sessel," the primary residence and workshop of the Froben family.</note>.
c. Paper expenses, also transport costs and the like.
d. Income in Basel and Frankfurt.
e. Expenses in Frankfurt.

---

<gloss>1</gloss> When page 50, 8 speaks of "brother-in-law Jeronymus Froben," the scribe does this, so to speak, in the place of the elder Nicolaus, who alone was responsible for settling accounts with Hieronymus Froben.

<gloss>2</gloss> Note how different types of currency are named and accounted for in these records. All sums received and spent in Basel, and specifically the reconciliations and final balances, are calculated in guilders <term>Gulden: A standard gold or silver currency used throughout the Holy Roman Empire; however, its value in smaller units like shillings varied by city.</term> of 25 shillings (either immediately or after conversion from pounds); all business from the Frankfurt Fair, by contrast, is calculated in guilders of 20 shillings. The fact that both are calculated as equivalent to each other, added, and subtracted, suggests that it was not the guilders themselves that differed in value, but rather the shillings.

<summary>This page identifies Nicolaus Episcopius the Younger as the ledger's scribe and firm's treasurer, using family relationships and his one-eighth profit share as evidence. It also outlines the ledger's structure, which was organized around the biannual Frankfurt book fairs and divided by workshop locations and currency types.</summary>

<keywords>Nicolaus Episcopius the Younger, Hieronymus Frobenius, bookkeeping, Basel, Frankfurt Fair, zum Luft, zum Sessel, currency conversion, Guilders, Shillings, printing expenses</keywords>

g. The debtors and their debts. (Assets <note>original: "Activa"</note>, which, however, are only settled in the subsequent accounts.)

Thus, the accounts for the Autumn Fair of 1558, the Autumn Fair of 1560, and both the Lenten and Autumn Fairs of 1561 are entirely missing; of those still extant, the Autumn Fair account of 1562 is the only one preserved in its entirety. However, those closest to it—the Lenten Fair of 1562 and the Lenten Fair of 1563—survive with so little damage that a nearly complete overview of these three half-year periods is possible. The remaining seven accounts offer only fragments.

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Froben-Episcopius partnership's internal organization, moving from the general structure of the account books to the specific roles and hierarchy of the family members involved.</meta>

The partnership was initially formed only by Hieronymus Froben and Nicolaus Episcopius the Elder, and only secondarily by their four sons (see page 18, line 11: "joint printing house of Froben and Episcopius, and also their sons," <note>original: "gmeyne truckerey Frobenii und Episcopii, auch yrer sünenn"</note> and page 20, line 4: "partnership of Froben and Episcopius, and also their associates" <note>original: "gmeyndtschafft Frobenii und Episcopii, auch yrer myttverwantenn"</note>). This dominant position of the two fathers is evident in several places, particularly in the distribution of business duties. However, the view that only the fathers were the true partners—because initially, they indeed were—is softened to the extent that the partnership is presented as a union of two groups, namely the two families. Froben and Episcopius together form the company <term>Societæt: A Latin term for a partnership or formal business association.</term>, with each side represented by its natural head, the father.

This relationship becomes clear through the entries in the ledger regarding the distribution of individual business transactions.

The sum total of these transactions lies in the hands of Hieronymus and the elder Nicolaus. They were the founders of the partnership, and the sons only gradually joined them. However, the primary right of decision and supreme management still remains with the fathers, at least according to the ledger. Here, only they are named when covering expenses ("Episcopius paid this" <note>original: "solchs zalt Episcopius"</note>, "Froben paid this" <note>original: "solchs zalt Frobenius"</note>)¹ and receiving income ("Episcopius took it in" <note>original: "Episcopius nam yn"</note>, "Froben took it in" <note>original: "Frobenius nam yn"</note>), and only they settle these accounts against one another (pp. 25, 32, 33, etc.). <meta>catchword: It</meta>

---

¹ This concerns both the expenses that each of the two elders incurs for the printing house based in his own home—that is, Froben for the **Workshop at the "Luft"** <note>The "Haus zum Luft" was the historic home and workshop of the Froben family.</note> and Episcopius for the **Workshop at the "Sessel"** <note>The "Haus zum Sessel" was the workshop associated with the Episcopius family, formerly the home of the printer Johann Amerbach.</note>—as well as other shared costs. Regarding the latter, it may be mentioned as a specific detail that when purchasing paper, the French merchants from Épinal are always paid by Nicolaus the Elder (only once was it someone else, Jerg Dyrr, p. 24, 24), while the papermakers from Basel and Maulburg deal with Hieronymus. Could it be that the latter did not speak the "Welsh" language <note>The original term "wälschen Sprache" refers here to Romance languages, specifically French.</note>? Episcopius, of course, had spent time in France during his youth.

<summary>This page describes the hierarchical structure of the Froben-Episcopius printing firm, explaining that despite the involvement of their sons, the two fathers maintained absolute control over finances and management. It also highlights a division of labor where Nicolaus Episcopius handled French-speaking paper merchants while Hieronymus Froben managed local German-speaking suppliers.</summary>

<keywords>Hieronymus Froben, Nicolaus Episcopius, business management, printing history, account books, paper trade, Basel, Épinal, Haus zum Luft, Haus zum Sessel</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the internal organization of the Froben-Episcopius printing partnership. While the previous page established the seniority of the two fathers, this page details the specific responsibilities of their sons—particularly in managing trade fair logistics—and clarifies the internal division of profits between the two families.</meta>

While there is no doubt that they are supported by their sons in all these activities, this is not taken into account at all in the bookkeeping.

The portion of the business activity which, even according to the ledger, is reserved for the younger partners <term>Gemeinschafter: Members of a business partnership, often involving family members in this period.</term> is traveling to the trade fairs. Only twice (p. 9, 10. 10, 33.) is it reported that the elder Nicolaus visited the fair at Frankfurt; Hieronymus Froben seems never to have been there. ¹ The sons had to make the journey all the more frequently, ² most often Nicolaus Episcopius the Younger (p. 9, 12. 10, 30. 23, 5. 30, 18. 49, 3. 66, 31.) and Ambrosius Froben (p. 9, 12. 10, 30. 30, 29. 49, 7. 61, 4. 66, 22.), then Aurelius Froben (p. 9, 10. 49, 8.) and once also Eusebius Episcopius (p. 67, 12).

The fact that the sons—despite not standing out prominently in the ledger—are indeed to be understood as partners <term>Gemeinder: A co-partner or fellow member of a business guild or company.</term> is clearly shown first by the frequently recurring formula "partnership between the Froben and Episcopius families" <note>original: "gemeinschaft zwischen Frobeniis und Episcopiis"</note>, and secondly by the fact that the writer of the book himself declares himself to be a partner (see above) and as such holds a share (see above), specifically one-eighth (1/8). In the common account, however, only the fifty-fifty split <term>Halbteilung: The fundamental division of the business into two equal halves, one for each founding patriarch's family.</term> is applied, but the distribution continues further within each half. It seems, therefore, that the elder Nicolaus held one-quarter (1/4) and each of his sons one-eighth (1/8) of the total result, and the shares may have been divided in the same way among the Froben family.

Exactly how much this total result amounted to in each case—in other words, how much the business yielded for the partners—cannot be determined from all the accounts provided in the ledger—

---

¹ Regarding his poor health, see above, p. 81.
² Which was not at all without danger <note>Travel in the 16th century involved significant risks from highwaymen, poor road conditions, and disease.</note>; see above in the main text p. 54, and what Felix Platter reports on p. 229 regarding the year 1554.

<summary>This page details the division of labor within the Froben-Episcopius partnership, noting that while the fathers managed the finances, the sons handled the dangerous but essential travel to the Frankfurt trade fairs. It also explains the profit-sharing structure, where the business was split equally between the two families before being further divided among the sons.</summary>

...evident. From the only complete account of the same, specifically that of autumn 1562, I extract the following items to clarify this point:

| | | fl. | sh. | dn. |
|:---|:---|---:|---:|---:|
| p. 39: | Expenses for the "Luft": <term>Zum Luft: The name of the house and printing office of the Froben family.</term> | 747. | 13. | 6. |

| p. 48, 26: | All expenses: | 3387. | 19. | 11. |
| | | 371. | 11. | 1. |

This results in a surplus of income amounting to the sum of 371 fl. 11 sh. 1 dn. <note>The currency units are Florins (fl.), Shillings (sh.), and Denarii or pennies (dn.).</note> In the same way, the cash balance for Lent 1562 <note>Lent refers to the spring trade fair in Frankfurt.</note> was 476. 22. 5. and for Lent 1563 it was 428. 24. 9. Accordingly, an average annual profit that the business could yield for each family can be assumed to be approximately 420 fl.; according to the estimates of Hanauer <gloss>1</gloss> this equals approximately 2100 Francs in today's money <note>This refers to the value at the time the author was writing in the late 19th century.</note>; a significant sum nonetheless, if one considers that the depreciation of the currency's value has not been taken into account here. In the aforementioned cash balances, unpaid outstanding debts remained unconsidered, as these arrears were roughly the same from one trade fair to the next and thus did not influence the final result.

<margin>Subordinate Staff.</margin>
In the service and pay of the community stood the workers, as numerous as the tasks necessary for the printing and publication of a book, and as varied in importance as these tasks were. On the one hand, there were the permanently employed proofreaders <note>original: "Correctoren"</note> (chastisers <note>original: "Castigatoren," from the Latin term for those who "punished" or corrected errors in a text.</note>), readers, typesetters, printers, and assistants; on the other—

Alongside the printers' wages, an expense item for <term>Pressenlohn</term> <note>original: "Pressenlohn," meaning "press-wage" or "press-labor costs."</note> regularly appears in the ledger; it is noteworthy that this press-wage is calculated according to <term>Tagewerke</term> <note>day-works or man-days</note>, the number of which in most cases corresponds to approximately half of the actual printers' day-works. Cf. p. 7, 2: "press-labor for a whole press." <note>original: "pressenlonn thůtt ein gantze press."</note> Here, it can surely only refer to the auxiliary personnel who stood alongside the printers and, as ink-appliers <note>Inkers who applied ink to the type before each impression.</note>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of labor costs and staffing at the Froben and Episcopius printing houses. The previous page ended while describing the "press-wage" and the workers who operated the printing presses.</meta>

...and so forth, who served the presses. Their wages amount consistently to a little more than half of the printer's wage.

Besides compositors and printers, other workers are naturally mentioned: mold-cutters <note>original: "Formenschneider," craftsmen who carved woodblocks for illustrations or decorative initials</note>, illustrators (provided they were on the permanent staff), as well as subordinate employees—an apprentice, someone who helped in the warehouse, and so on. Not to mention the carters and boatmen who were only hired temporarily.

The difference between the <term>Corrector</term> <note>In this period, a proofreader who often had significant editorial responsibilities</note> and the <term>Lector</term> <note>A scholarly assistant or "reader"</note> is described by *Theodor Zwinger*¹ in such a way that among the intellectual workers <note>original: "operae theoricae"</note> engaged in book printing, the *Correctors* are the principals <note>original: "principes"</note> and the *Lectors* are the assistants <note>original: "famulantes"</note>. According to our account book, the activity of the *Corrector* consists of correction <note>original: "correctio"</note>, refinement <note>original: "castigatio"</note>, inspection <note>original: "recognitio"</note> (p. 6, 13.), indexing <note>original: "index"</note> (p. 8, 8.; 56, 13.; 58, 11.), and preparation <note>original: "zůrůrsten," likely referring to the "make-ready" process of preparing a form for the press</note> (58, 10.). The activity of the *Lector* consists of reading <note>original: "lectio"</note>, refinement <note>original: "castigatio"</note> (p. 6, 17.), collation <note>original: "collatio," the process of comparing different versions of a text</note> (p. 8, 35.), inspection <note>original: "recognitio"</note> (p. 20, 20.), and indexing <note>original: "index"</note> (p. 20, 19.).² The *Corrector* of that time corresponds roughly to the modern printing house manager <note>original: "Buchdruckerfactor"</note>, while the *Lector* of that time corresponds to the modern proofreader.

Of a similar nature, though measured on a much larger scale, was the work of the otherwise independent scholars recruited for scientific collaboration, whose names are mentioned in the account book: *Philippus Bechius*, *Ulricus Coccius*, *Concenatius*, *Conradus Gesnerus* <note>Conrad Gessner, the famous Swiss naturalist and bibliographer</note>, *Jacobus Hertelius*, *Sebastianus Lepusculus*, and *Voxius*.³ Their number does not seem large compared to the works published during this time by the *Frobens* and *Episcopiuses*.

¹ <note>original: "Methodus apodemica," a work by Theodor Zwinger on the art of travel and observation</note> p. 399.

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the workforce and payroll at the Basel printing houses of Froben and Episcopius during the 16th century. The previous page defined the roles of the intellectual workers—the Corrector and the Lector—distinguishing between the primary editorial staff and their scholarly assistants.</meta>

However, it should be considered that some of these works were merely new printings of earlier editions, for which the <term>Corrector</term> <note>A scholarly proofreader responsible for the accuracy of the text</note> and <term>Lector</term> <note>A scholarly assistant or reader</note> were already capable of performing the necessary work.

<margin>Honorariums and wages</margin>
What the scholars received as honorariums for their work is evident from several passages; the scale applied can be seen by comparing the paid sum with the work itself—for example, compare page 7, line 9; page 39, lines 5 and 14; page 41, line 5; and page 57, line 14 with the corresponding details in the notes.

Episcopius <note>Nikolaus Episcopius the Younger (1531–1564), a prominent Basel printer and partner of the Froben family</note> calculates the wages of the Correctors and Lectors sometimes according to the scope of the work they handled, and sometimes according to the time spent working; in the latter calculation, at "the Air" <note>original: "zum Luft," referring to a specific printing press or workshop area named "The Air"</note>, a Corrector received 30 shillings a week, and a Lector 20 shillings; at "the Chair" <note>original: "zum Sessel," another specific press or workshop name</note>, a Corrector received between 26 shillings 8 pennies and 40 shillings per week.

The other weekly wages appear equally varied:

->At the Air:<-

A compositor: 36 — 60 shillings
A printer: 28 shillings 6 pennies — 33 shillings 3 pennies

->At the Chair:<-

A compositor: 39 shillings 11 pennies — 40 shillings
A printer: 28 shillings 5 pennies — 33 shillings

The Corrector and Lector were thus paid less than the most skilled <term>compositors</term> <note>Workers who manually set the lead type</note> and printers.¹

---

¹ Aside from the differing duration of their employment, the differences in the earnings of the compositors are primarily based on their general work proficiency; furthermore, in special cases, they are explained by the fact that different types of typesetting were paid differently—specifically, Greek and Hebrew typesetting were more expensive than Latin or German. However, in most cases, this cannot be proven because the account book only specifies the duration of work for the compositors in only two accounts, page 72 and page 74. In the earlier overviews, we lack this information to find the scale of the calculation. For example, on page 8, lines 11 and 12, the difference between the wage for Greek typesetting and that for...

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of wage records for Basel printing houses, specifically comparing the pay rates for different types of linguistic labor—such as Latin versus Hebrew typesetting—and the accounting practices for craftsmen.</meta>

...the Latin works are certainly striking, but since no records of the working hours are included, they cannot be explained in a way that allows for firm conclusions to be drawn. We are on firmer ground on page 72, where lines 22 and 23 provide clear evidence; it concerns the same compositor <note>A worker who sets the type for the printing press</note>, Joannes Parcus, who earns—either through piece-rate calculation or as a fixed sum—10 shillings for the Hebrew dictionary <note>original: "dictionarium"</note> and 6 shillings for the Latin Gregory <note>original: "Gregorius," likely referring to the works of Pope Gregory the Great</note>. Perhaps it should also be considered that a compositor might perform a job in partnership with others, but settle the accounts with the master <note>original: "Chef"</note> only for himself, and thus he alone was listed in the ledger—a practice that still occurs today.

It would have been valuable to also find the calculations underlying the fees paid to artists and <term>block-cutters</term> <note>original: "Formschneider"; skilled craftsmen who carved the designs of artists into woodblocks for printing</note>; unfortunately, the precise material for this is lacking. For where the services of such artists and their payments are listed, it is not done with the specificity required to reliably identify these same contributions within the printed works themselves and thereby determine the scale of the wages.

L. 19: Petrus Perna (also Pernas, Bernus), from Lucca, as he himself states; recorded as "from Löckh" <note>a German phonetic spelling of Lucca</note> in the citizenship register, "of Lucca" <note>original: "Lucensis"</note> in his epitaph, and "from Bergeman" in the guild admission roll, was born before 1522.¹ On February 28, 1542, he enrolled as a student in the Basel <term>matriculation register</term> <note>the official list of students at the university</note>, but paid nothing because of poverty <note>original: "propter inopiam"</note>. After this, he turned to the book trade and took over the equipment necessary for printing from Thomas Platter.² In 1548, he petitioned the City Council for citizenship, but was rejected,

---

¹ According to the grave inscription, Gross 151, Tonjola 132.
² Thomas Platter 104.

<summary>This page provides scholarly annotations and corrections for a 16th-century ledger, focusing on identifying individuals like the printer Petrus Perna and clarifying geographic and mathematical discrepancies in the records.</summary>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical and editorial commentary on Basel printers from the 16th century, specifically following the accounts related to Peter Perna and moving into the history of Thomas Guarinus.</meta>

because he could not provide proof of his legal standing <note>original: "Mannrecht"; likely referring to proof of legitimate birth or marital status required for citizenship</note>. In order to obtain this, he traveled to Italy, his homeland. However, because he carried Protestant <note>original: "evangelische"</note> books with him, he was imprisoned. He seems to have been held there for some time, for it was not until June 8, 1555, that he applied for citizenship in Basel again.^1 But only after petitioning a second time was he accepted as a citizen on June 9, 1557,^2 and he then joined the Saffron Guild <note>one of the traditional trade guilds in Basel, representing merchants and later printers</note> on November 14 of the same year.^3 He was married to Johanna Vercaska, who died in 1580. Two years later, on August 16, 1582, he also died; he was buried at St. Peter's.^4 Peter Perna was both a book printer and a bookseller; however, the significance and extent of his activities in these roles have yet to be fully determined. His printer's mark (and later that of Conrad Waldkirch) is a standing woman carrying a burning lamp in her right hand, with the words: *Thy word is a lamp unto my feet* <note>original: "lucerna pedibus meis verbum tuum"; a reference to Psalm 119:105</note>. Alluding to this, Perna's epitaph says of him: *whose typographic lamp was lit by the lamp of the most holy religion* <note>original: "cuius lucernam typographicam ss. religionis lucerna accendit"</note>.

Regarding P. 5, L. 3: Thomas Guarinus, Guerinus, Gering, of Dorneck, Dornyck, Tournay (according to the citizenship book and epitaph), of Antwerp <note>original: "Andorff"</note> (according to the guild enrollment list), was born in 1529.^5 For the sake of his faith, he left his fatherland^6 and went to Lyon, where he ran a bookshop and continued to maintain it even later.^7 In 1557, he was in Basel and married Elisabeth, the daughter of the master printer Michael Isengrin "at the Falcon."^8 Isengrin died in March of that year; on November 3, Guarinus received citizenship^9 and on November 14, at the same time as Peter Perna, he received his guild rights for the Saffron Guild.^10 After his father-in-law's death, he took over the printing office <note>original: "Officin"</note>, initially probably with the widow^11 and later alone, which then operated under his name. With the office, the old printer's mark of Bebelius, the palm tree, also passed to him.

Guarinus died on May 6, 1592, and was buried in the Cathedral <note>the Basel Münster</note>. The beautiful monument shared by him and his parents-in-law, featuring the coat of arms of Isengrin <note>Michael Isengrin, a famous Basel printer; his mark depicts a wolf</note> (which is identical to his printer's mark) and the house mark of Guarinus, is located in the Cathedral cloister on the exterior wall of the St. Nicholas Chapel.

<margin>Regarding page 6.</margin> Line 4 is written in red in the manuscript. Line 14: Is 58 corrected from 54?
Line 9: ***House of the Air.*** <note>original: "Haus zum Lufft"</note> Today Bäumleingasse 18 and Luftgässlein 2 and 4. The house formerly belonged to the "zu Rhyn" estate. It was not until June 14, 1531, that Hieronymus Frobenius and his wife Anna Lachnerinn purchased it from Anthoni von Sygolzheym, a citizen of Strasbourg, and his wife Ursula Meygerinn, for 750 Guilders <note>original: "Gld."; a standard gold coin of the era</note>.^1 Johann Froben, therefore, did not own this house, as Schweighauser assumes,^2 and Erasmus only lived in it after his return from Freiburg; it was also here that he died. Theodor Zwinger calls the house: the windy house, "At the Air," the workshop of the Frobens, ennobled by the life and death of Desiderius Erasmus <note>original: "domus ventosa, zum Lufft, Frobeniorum officina, Des. Erasmi vita et morte nobilitata"</note>.^3 Regarding the possibility that Nicolaus Episcopius also lived in the "Air" from 1531 until before 1543, see above, page 85.

Line 7. St. John's House <note>original: "Johanserhuss"</note>. This is understood to be the former Froben—now Froben and Episcopius—book warehouse in the St. Johann suburb, today No. 24 there. On November 17, 1522, Master Hans Froben and his wife Gertrud Lachner bought a grain granary <note>original: "Kornschütte"</note> in the suburb "at the Cross on the Rhine bank" <note>original: "ze crütz an der Rheinhalde"</note> from the Petri siblings.² From this barn, Froben created his book warehouse, which also served his son and his business partners <note>original: "Gemeinder"</note> as a storage place for larger stocks of books. In 1581, it was called Eusebius Bischoff’s Book House <note>original: "Eusebii Bischoffs Bůchhus"</note>, in 1602 the Episcopian Book House <note>original: "Episcopianisches Bůchhus"</note>, and even today it is simply referred to by the name Book House <note>original: "Bůchhus"</note>. It was adjacent to the houses of Hans Holbein.³

Line 8. The term "inschlagen" <note>literally "wrapping" or "packing in"</note> the barrels should probably be understood as the packing of books into them for shipment, a task for which a bookbinder was quite well-suited. At the very least, a Friedrich Rode appears during that time as a bookbinder. He was a native of Pfedersen, became a citizen on July 16, 1544, and a member of the Saffron Guild <note>one of the merchant guilds of Basel</note> on July 20. In the years 1547, 1548, and 1550, he appears with his wife Catharina in the baptismal register of St. Peter's, as well as in 1556 and 1561 with his (second) wife Margredt Holtzachin. Line 3. Regarding the "Sessel" <note>the house "Zum Sessel" or "At the Chair," the historic home and workshop of the Froben press</note>. Today located at Totengässlein 3. The following are named to us as previous owners of this house: 1360 Greda Sürlin. 1369 Heintzeman Murer. Bernhard Sevogel. Veronica Sevogel. 1488 Mathias Eberler.² Toward the end of the 15th century, the house came to Johann Amerbach; after his death in 1514, it passed to Johann Froben, who kept and lived in it until the end of his life.³ In 1527, when he died, his widow Gertrud seems to have remained in the house; Hieronymus Froben lived in the house "Arow." In 1529, the son-in-law Nicolaus Episcopius also moved into the "Sessel" with his young wife.⁴ However, Froben’s widow seems to have lived here even after her remarriage; at the very least, Herwagen is still called the "Master Printer at the Sessel" in 1537.⁵ In 1531, Nicolaus Episcopius likely left the house "Zum Sessel" and moved with his brother-in-law and now fellow...

<meta>This page continues the historical notes on the ownership and residents of the prominent "House at the Sign of the Chair" (Zum Sessel) in Basel, as well as bibliographic details regarding associates and workers of the Froben press.</meta>

...citizen Hieronymus Froben moved to the House "at the Sign of the Air" <note>German: "Haus zum Luft"</note>. Later, at the beginning of the 1540s, he seems to have returned to "The Chair" and lived and worked here until his death. His sons and grandsons also occupied the house. At the beginning of the 17th century, it passed to Leonhard Respinger. Today, it houses the higher girls' school.

Line 7. Laurentius Hunfredus <note>Laurence Humphrey (1527–1589), an English theologian and President of Magdalen College, Oxford</note>. A dedication by him to the reader <note>original Latin: "ad lectorem"</note> is found in Forster’s Hebrew Lexicon of 1557 (see below); the same dedication as well as a Latin poem by him appear in the 1564 edition of the same book (see below); in 1563, he provided a commentary on Cyril (see below).

Line 9. Leodigarius Grimaldus (Grymoult), of Rouen, enrolled at the University of Basel as a student in the year 1551 during the rectorate of Bonifacius Amerbach. He was married to Anna Doana; Nicolaus Episcopius <note>a prominent Basel printer and partner to the Froben family</note> served as the godfather to his son Petrus, who was baptized at St. Peter’s on August 7, 1558. <note>1</note> In the Basel Public Library (Cod. G. II. 17.), there are two letters from him: to Bonifacius Amerbach dated Bologna, March 1, 1556, and to Basilius Amerbach dated July 4, 1567.

Line 11. Joannes Parcus. This name appears 6 times in the account book: 1. Joannes Parcus, <term>typesetter</term> <note>original: "Setzer"</note> at "The Chair," March 1558, p. 8. 2. Joannis Parcus's son, typesetter at "The Chair," March 1558, p. 8. 3. Joannes Parcus, also called Touttin, typesetter at "The Air," March 1560, p. 18. 4. 5. 6. Joannes Parcus, typesetter at "The Air," Autumn 1562 — March 1564, pp. 38, 56, 72. Regarding the relationship of these names to one another, I suspect the following: numbers 2 through 6 refer to the same person, the son of number 1. It can be assumed with certainty that numbers 3 through 6 are identical, as they always refer to a typesetter in the workshop <note>original: "Officin"</note> "at the Sign of the Air." Given this, it would be striking if the younger Johannes Parcus, mentioned as a typesetter in March 1558, never appeared again. It is probable that his father died in the meantime and he then moved to the other workshop. It is also possible that these individuals named Parcus might have been known by the German name Kündig, as is established for Jacob Parcus, who was likely their relative; <note>2</note> the elder typesetter Joannes Parcus...

**Line 13:** Mathias von der Hecken, the printer, from the territory of Jülich, became a citizen of Basel on September 8, 1548.<sup>2</sup> However, he was not admitted to the Saffron Guild <note>one of the influential trade guilds in Basel</note> until April 20, 1569.<sup>3</sup> He was married to Anna Vögelin and died in 1577.<sup>4</sup> Compare with Christoph von der Hecken on page 117 below.

<meta>This page transitions from the bibliographic description of the 1562 Galen edition to a series of critical editorial notes and historical annotations regarding account books. It specifically addresses financial corrections and a historical dispute over customs duties affecting the Basel book trade.</meta>

Line 25: The addition total is incorrect; it should read 370. 9. 9.

Regarding page 21, line 7: As a result of the calculation error made on page 20, line 25, this sum is incorrect; it should read 604. 1. 1. = 483 <term>fl.: florins or guilders, the standard currency for trade</term> 6. 1.

Regarding page 22, line 19: 425 is corrected over 245. Line 25: Manuscript: <note>original: "vochenn," an archaic spelling of "Wochen"</note> "weeks." Line 32: 530 corrected over 386.

Line 1: Psalm 101, verses 3 and 4.

Line 16: Christoffel von der Hecken became a citizen of Basel in 1561.<sup>1</sup>

Line 19: The original number 245 was incorrect; the 425 added through correction is correct.

Line 31: The summation was made before the correction, but then the correction of the total was omitted. In contrast, in line 32, the total in guilders, originally 386, was correctly amended to 530.

Regarding page 23: Lines 3, 8, and 9 are underlined in red. Line 24: 530 corrected over 386. Line 26: 724 corrected over 580.

Line 6: Concenatius <note>likely referring to the legal scholar Gianantonio Conti, known as Concenatius</note>, editor of Bartolus <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato, a highly influential medieval jurist</note>, see below, page 118.

Line 8: Customs officer at Biesheim. Biesheim is a small town located in Alsace, in the district of Colmar, Canton of Neubreisach. Here ran the road intended as the primary trade route for travelers and goods moving toward the Frankfurt Fair (hence it was simply called the "Frankfurt Road") or further north. The fact that the main traffic moved along the left bank of the Rhine and used the road located there was likely because there were fewer sovereign territories and therefore fewer <term>Zollstätten: customs houses</term> to pass than on the other side of the river. However, in the year 1559, new customs duties had been established in the Further Austrian lands, to which Biesheim belonged; the Confederates <note>The Swiss Confederacy</note> filed a complaint about this with the Emperor<sup>2</sup>, and Basel in particular was prompted to enter negotiations due to a new customs duty levied at Biesheim on Basel merchants. Specifically, this was the "book tax" recorded at this point in the account book, against which a number of Basel booksellers—led by our Nicolaus Episcopius—filed a <term>Supplication: a formal petition or humble request</term> with the City Council.<sup>3</sup> It is undated, but a comparison with the Council’s correspondence regarding this matter shows that it was written between the 7th and 10th of September.

<sup>3</sup> Basel State Archive, L. 151. B. 2.
