<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the customs dispute at Biesheim and provides detailed bibliographic entries for legal and classical works mentioned in the account books, specifically focusing on editions of Plutarch and Bartolus de Saxoferrato from the early 1560s.</meta>

...entered in March 1561. These letters from the Council are as follows: March 10, 1561: to Peter Wadröß, customs officer at Biesheim. March 10: to the government at Ensisheim. March 15: to Peter Wadröß, customs officer at Biesheim. March 17: to the same. April 5: to the same. April 7: to Lux Gebhart.¹ It is not to be assumed, however, that the efforts of the Council—which were directed toward the abolition of this toll, or at least the suspension of its collection until a decision was reached in this matter—were met with success. At the very least, our account book shows that Froben and Episcopius <note>Hieronymus Froben and Nicolaus Episcopius were prominent Basel printers and business partners</note> still had to pay customs duties on their book crates at Biesheim in November 1561, March 1562, and September 1562.

Line 11: Plutarchus. The book meant here seems to be the one which Georg Tanner described in 1565 (Stintzing, *Tanner's Letters* 63) as: "*Plutarch’s Lives of Illustrious Men, translated by Hermann Cruserius, Doctor of Laws, in folio, published at the expense of Oporinus and Episcopius.*" <note>original Latin: "Plutarchi vitæ virorum illustrium Hermanno Cruserio J. C. interprete in fol. impensis Oporini et Episcopii."</note> I do not dare to decide whether this is the following work: "*The Comparative Lives of Illustrious Men by Plutarch, translated by Hermann Cruserius, Doctor of Laws. In folio. Basel, at the house of Thomas Guarinus, August 1564.*" <note>original Latin: "Plutarchi vitæ comparatæ illustrium virorum Hermanno Cruserio J. C. interprete. in fol. Basileæ apud Thomam Guarinum 1564, mense Augusto."</note> (Public Library of Basel. B. c. I. 86), but I suspect it is, because I have been unable to find any other edition. Even if the year of printing does not match the account book, the preface by Cruserius is already dated from 1561.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Basel’s industrial history, moving from the Hüsler family to the Dürr family, who were central to the city's papermaking industry in the 16th century. It provides genealogical data and reconciles financial records from the account books.</meta>

Line 24: Jerg Dürr. Surpassing the Hüsler family in prominence was the Dürr family of **papermakers**. During this period, they held the same significance that the Gallician family held in the 15th century, to whom they were also related by marriage. The following are the individual members, and their relationships to one another are presumably as follows: 1. **Jerg <note>A South German diminutive for Georg or George.</note> Dürr** of Reutlingen, the papermaker; purchased membership in the Saffron Guild on July 2, 1508,¹ became a citizen on January 7, 1511,² and was married to Veronica Gallician.³ His son: 2. **Georgius Dürr**, the papermaker; renewed his Saffron Guild membership on January 13, 1527. Certainly a close relative, probably a nephew of Jerg Dürr, was: 3. **Niclaus Dürr** of Reutlingen, the papermaker; purchased Saffron Guild membership on January 13, 1527, and citizenship on February 25, 1527.⁴ His son: 5. **Claus Dürr**, the papermaker; renewed Saffron Guild membership on September 24, 1553, and died on June 2, 1573.⁵ The son of Georgius was: 4. **Jerg Dürr**, the papermaker; renewed Saffron Guild membership on August 5, 1543, and died on June 26, 1570.⁶ His son: 6. **Niclaus Dürr the younger**, the papermaker; renewed Saffron Guild membership on February 25, 1565. Additionally, the following members whom I am unable to place in the lineage: 7. **Jeronimus Dürr**, the papermaker; renewed Saffron Guild membership on September 6, 1570. 8. **Niclaus Dürr the young**, the papermaker; renewed Saffron Guild membership on March 11, 1593. The individual named in the account book is No. 4. The house and workshop of the Dürr family, like those of the Gallicians, were located at St. Alban <term>im Loch</term> <note>Literally "in the hollow," a district in Basel famous for its water-powered paper mills.</note>.

Line 26: **Epinal**, France, department of Vosges. An Antoni, <term>bappirmacher</term> <note>Archaic German for papermaker.</note> at Spinal <note>The Latin/Old German name for Epinal.</note>, perhaps a relative of the Gallicians, is mentioned as early as 1480: *Waiver Book 414*.

Line 20: 425. 24. 6. corrected over 407. 18. 3.

Line 11: Vincentius Prallus, born in Hamburg on August 1, 1537, likely as the son of the organist Valentin Pralle.¹ In the year 1560, he matriculated at the University of Basel and was thereafter active as a corrector <note>term: corrector; a learned editor in a printing house responsible for proofreading and correcting texts in Latin, Greek, and Hebrew</note>, in which position our account book mentions him. He later became the pastor at Hüningen, then at St. Peter.² On June 18, 1572, he received citizenship³, and on June 22, [he was admitted to] the Saffron Guild.⁴ In 1578, he succeeded Thomas Platter in the school rectorate,⁵ became Professor of Eloquence <note>original German: "Beredsamkeit"</note> in 1590,⁶ and died on April 28, 1594.⁷ His wife was Barbara Schmid, born 1538, died 1604.⁸

<meta>This page concludes the bibliographic entry for the 1565 Basel edition of Justin Martyr's works, providing details on the colophon and identifying the true printer of the volume.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>

-> [AMBR]OSE AND [AURELI]US <note>original: "BENI- OS"; this is the end of the Latinized name "Frobenios," referring to the Froben brothers.</note> | BROTHERS: | IN THE YEAR of human salvation 1565. | In the month of March. | *Finally, the Froben printer's mark on a separate leaf.* <-

-> *Public Library of Basel. F. K. IV. 10.* <-

*According to the account book, this Justinus <note>Referring to the works of Justin Martyr described on the previous page.</note> was printed by Oporinus <note>Johannes Oporinus (1507–1568) was a renowned Basel printer and scholar, famous for printing the first Latin translation of the Qur'an and Vesalius’s landmark work on anatomy.</note> at the expense of the firm, from which it was then taken over by the Froben brothers. Of course, this printer is nowhere named in the book, but as it seems to me, he is recognizable by the difference from the products of the Froben-Episcopius workshops <note><term>Officinen</term>: from the Latin "officina," meaning a workshop or printing house.</note>, particularly in the style of the decorative initials. The fact that the Froben signet <note><term>Signet</term>: a printer's mark or brand, often a woodcut illustration, used to identify the press or the publisher.</note> is nonetheless attached to the work shows that these emblems served as publisher’s marks just as much as printer’s marks.*

<image-desc>A line-art illustration, likely a woodcut, depicting a winged putto seen from the back and slightly from the right. The nude figure hangs suspended in the air, holding onto a large circular ring with both hands. The figure has small wings and its left leg is slightly tucked.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes the description of a 1565 edition of Justin Martyr, noting that although published by the Froben brothers, the physical printing was likely done by Johannes Oporinus. It explains the dual role of printer's marks as both house brands and publisher logos.</summary>

## LIST OF WOODCUTS.

1) Page III. <term>Printer's device</term> <note>a symbolic logo used by early printers to identify their work</note> of Heinrich Petri, former owner of the same <term>printing house</term> <note>original German: "Officin"</note> in which the present book was printed.
2) Page V. Border strip, after a drawing by Niklaus Manuel.
3) Page VII. <term>Tailpiece</term> <note>a decorative ornament placed at the end of a chapter or page</note>, after a drawing by Hans Holbein in *The Praise of Folly* <note>the famous satirical essay by Erasmus</note>.
4) Page 1. Printer's device of the Froben family <note>prominent Basel printers associated with Erasmus</note>.
5) Page 76. Printer's device of the Episcopius family.
6) Page 77. <term>Headpiece</term> <note>a decorative element at the top of a page</note>, from the title border of a print by Adam Petri. After Holbein, woodcut by Hans Lützelburger.
7) Page 79. Border strip, after a drawing by Niklaus Manuel.
8) Page 100. Tailpiece, from the title border used on page 77.
9) Page 135. Tailpiece, after a pen drawing by an unknown artist; part of a goldsmith's <term>design</term> <note>original German: "Risses," referring to a technical sketch or blueprint</note>.
10) Page 150. Printer's device of Adam Petri.

The originals of nos. 2, 3, 6, 7, 8, and 9 are located in the Public Art Collection in Basel.

<meta>This page serves as the colophon, the concluding statement of the publication. <page-type>colophon</page-type>

<image-desc>A large rectangular woodcut printer's mark. In the center, a nude child or putto is riding a maned lion. The child holds a thin staff that extends vertically behind him, forming a tall cross at the top. Draped over the cross is a fluttering banner divided into two parts; the left side contains the Christogram "IHS" <note>a abbreviation of the name of Jesus in Greek</note> and the right side contains the letters "ADP" <note>the initials of Adam Petri, the 16th-century Basel printer whose heritage this firm claimed</note>. The central figures are surrounded by climbing rose bushes with large blooms. The entire scene is set within an architectural frame consisting of two decorated columns supporting a rounded arch. The arch is draped with floral garlands, and small winged cherub heads occupy the upper spandrels.</image-desc>
